It is actually quite strong, I think, that at Christiansborg, where there are many things that share in everyday life, we can build bridges and create a sustainable solution to such an important policy area. It is perhaps something unique about the Danish people's rule that we sometimes forget and which I would therefore like to draw forward here for a start, because politics is often about focusing on differences and disagreements, but we actually have a strong tradition of building bridges. We have that in Christiansborg, and we have that in society as such. I just came from the first meeting of the Disruption Council, which is another example of this unique Danish tradition that we work together across. It was a good meeting which reflects the broad composition of the partnership: ministers, employees' representatives, businesses, entrepreneurs, young people, because the challenges we face are of course affecting all of us, and that is why we need to find the answers together. We have a good starting point in Denmark, and our ambition is to ensure that Denmark is also one of the richest countries when we come up with a generation. The work we have started runs until 2018, but it is crucial for me that it is not a jar of jelly, but rather a catalyst for ideas and political debate. I look forward to this discussion on how Denmark will move forward into the future, also being conducted here in the Folketing, when we are to implement together the policy that will lead Denmark into the future. The housing agreement is a good example of how we can actually find things that collect and not always focus only on what shares. At this meeting we have just held, there was also a general consensus that Denmark has a good starting point, and we have this, among other things, because we have many strengths, and one of the most important is the trust we have in each other and our public institutions. This may not always be what is left of when you follow the public debate, but the fact is that if you make an international comparison, the Danes are among the most trusting in relation to the public sector. After all, it is a trust that it has taken generations to build, but which can be broken down in a split second, and if that happens, the foundations of our common welfare society crumble. It is therefore also very serious that changing governments and numerous ministers from my own party, the Social Democrats, Radicals and the SF have not managed to create the framework for the smooth functioning of the SKAT. The tax minister also promised that yesterday, and I would like to reiterate that commitment here today. That is why the Minister of Taxation will now call the parties for a debate, so that, at the same time, I try to stress the word at the same time, because I think it is absolutely crucial that we do not now choose to have so much focus in the rearview mirror that we forget to look out the windshield. It is thus out of the windshield that the future is being created and the confidence that has been challenged must be rebuilt, and it is now best done by ensuring that SKAT will function optimally again. That's the key to regaining confidence.

ParlaMint-DK_20170516140415	The debate is primarily concerned with the proposed amendments, and that is Mr Beazley's point of view. Pelle Dragsted, who wishes to speak. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20170516140441	Thank you, President. I will be brief about this bill, as I expect to speak on the next item on the pension situation of Members of Parliament. I would just like to explain the amendment tabled here by Enhedslisten for the third reading of this proposal. It is about removing the new possibility of a so-called early retirement, which is being introduced with this bill, i.e. an early retirement for ministers. We were actually very happy when we read that some time ago the parties to the agreement had agreed that in future the retirement age of ministers should follow the retirement age. This is something we have been working for for many years. We think it should be a matter of course that, when we change the retirement age for the population at Christiansborg, those rules must of course also apply to ourselves, including those of us who are fortunate enough to become ministers. That is why we were all the more disappointed when it came to the conclusion that the parties to the agreement apparently had such a clever trick introduced at the last minute, which in fact means that a minister will still be able to retire in the future before most other Danes. It is not the kind of early retirement that we know from the rest of society, which is virtually impossible to obtain, because we have to go through one work test after another and run a mess between different authorities. No, this is an optional early retirement, which you can choose freely as you like. This means that you can retire up to 5 years before the retirement age against a minor deduction in your pension. The scheme combined with the very rapid recovery of pension • a minister earns full pension in just 8 years • means that ministers will be able to retire with a really good pension several years before the retirement age. We do not think that is fair. It must be the case that the rules that apply to the rest of the population also apply to us, in other words to the politicians who have adopted them. It is hardly the case, however, that being a politician or a minister is more grueling than many of the jobs that other Danes contest, without being given the opportunity to retire before the retirement age. That is why we have tabled an amendment to the draft law which removes this possibility of so-called early retirement, so that in future ministers will be covered by the same rules on retirement age as ordinary employees in the labour market. You will then still have a very, very lucrative and quickly earned pension, but you cannot raise it until the retirement age.
ParlaMint-DK_20170516140736	Thank you. Then the Minister for Finance has asked to speak. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20170516140746	I should just like to thank very briefly the broad majority of the Danish Parliament, which supports the changes that make the retirement age that ministers have made in the future equal to what it is in the ordinary labour market. It is precisely an equality with the ordinary labour market, which also applies to the question that Mr. Pelle Dragsted travels on the possibility of early retirement. It is true that in both the ordinary, contractual and civil service labour market there is the possibility of retiring earlier than at the retirement age fixed by the Folketing, against a deduction in its pension. This means, in turn, that you get reduced what you get paid on a regular basis. Since, at the time of age, ministers' pension conditions are not applied for at the time of their retirement but at the time of their retirement, there is also, according to the agreement, the possibility of obtaining an early retirement pension scheme. I would like to make it clear that this is not something extraordinary, it is not something unknown. It's not like it's completely new and we're the only ones who've had the chance to do this before. It is the case that a large number of officials already have the opportunity to do so; it is the case that members of parliament have the opportunity to do so, and it is also the case that a large number of those on collective occupational pension schemes can receive them earlier, against a deduction in the amount paid. So it is an equality which the parties have chosen, also in relation to the possibility of stopping a little earlier on from going into retirement.

ParlaMint-DK_20170516141930	Thank you very much. The proposal we are now dealing with third-reading is about the pension situation of members of parliament, where the proposal we were discussing earlier was about the pension situation of ministers. For many years, the Enhedslisten has been working to normalise the pension situation of politicians, so that they are fleeing to a greater extent than they are today with the conditions of other Danes. We don't think politicians should have any violent special privileges. We think it should be paid fairly and so on. and so on, but we do not think that there should be excessive special privileges. The proposal that is being adopted today contains some progress, but it is not at all enough to put an end to some of the very far-reaching privileges that politicians enjoy. In fact, some of us run the risk of gaining greater privileges than today; that is, the rules become more lucrative. That is why, both at second reading and at third reading, we have tabled a number of amendments on the part of the Enhedslisten and, if adopted, they will help to create more reasonable conditions which are more similar to those of other Danes. This bill means that in future politicians will also have a very extraordinary pension scheme, where in a few years and much faster than almost all other Danes earn the right to a very high pension. Unfortunately, the majority of the parties to the contract have chosen to overhear the proposals of the Service Commission, which was otherwise set up to switch to a more moderate pension more similar to that of most employees. The Folketing now has the chance to rectify this, because the SF has retabled the amendment we tabled at second reading to simply follow the recommendations of the remuneration committee and move to a savings-based labour market pension. Unfortunately, it seems that a majority will again vote against. The draft law here means, of course, raising the retirement age for members of parliament, so that in future it will follow the retirement age rather than, as it is today, the early retirement age. That's good enough. However, as I mentioned in my previous speech, this little trick has been introduced with a so-called early retirement option. It is therefore not such an early retirement pension as we know from the general world outside Christiansborg, where you just as well have to have a medical cause and be approved in order to receive an early retirement pension. No, it's the kind of early retirement you choose freely if you want it to be. And combined with the very lucrative pension, where you earn the highest pension in 20 years, it will mean that members of parliament can also retire in the future with a very high pension several years before the retirement age...(Sound from the Chamber President: And you can even get this early retirement pension, while you have a different job and a high income. We in the Enhedslist believe that this is not fair and we have therefore also tabled an amendment which removes this possibility of early retirement. Let us then turn to the amendment tabled by the Social Democratic Party that members of parliament should be able to opt for a more moderate pension optionally. In fact, as I said at second reading, I think this is an embarrassing round on the part of the Socialist top, because instead of listening to the people's hero, I think, fair criticism of the politicians' pension situation or just following the commission, that is, the remuneration commission, which you have set up yourself, you are making this suggestion that those who are dissatisfied with their far too high pensions can simply go down on their own. But this whole debate on the pension situation of politicians is not about some being dissatisfied with their pensions. It is about the fact that many in the population are dissatisfied with the fact that we have a pension that is so lucrative in relation to what ordinary people have. And the fact that some of us then choose to go down on a lower pension will not make the discontent of the population disappear if the rest retains those privileges. However, as you know, in the Enhedslisten we have chosen to support the Social Democratic proposal that you can voluntarily renounce this privilege pension and move on to a more moderate pension. And we have announced that our members of the Folketing will also move on to this lower pension if that option comes. It is not because we think it is a good proposal; as I said, we do not think so. But it is because we hope or have a little hope that such a scheme might put pressure on the politicians who choose the privilege pension to go to their constituency, to their constituents, and explain why they think they should have such a lucrative pension, which really means that they can retire several years before most other Danes. Therefore, if there is to be voluntary service, it is of course essential that there should also be full transparency as to which parties and politicians maintain the privilege pension and who voluntarily grants it. That is why we have also tabled an amendment to the Social Democrats' proposal, which will ensure transparency, so that the people have the opportunity to follow who thinks they should have pension relationships similar to those of the population and who believes they have the right to far more lucrative relationships. But again: a voluntary scheme is not the solution to the dissatisfaction that exists in the population about our pension situation. The very idea of the Social Democrats' proposal that politicians should move to a pension scheme that reflects the civil servant pension so that in future it will take 37 years to earn a full pension against today, when it only takes 20 years to earn a full pension. And that is why we have also taken the Social Democrats' proposal and tabled an amendment that we simply implement it for all members of the Folketing with phasing-in from the next parliamentary term. It really surprises me that the Social Democrats are apparently not going to support the proposal that they themselves have put forward in this version, in which it covers themselves, but will only support it if it is optional. But it may be that there is a rapporteur who will come up here and explain it. To conclude, I would just like to say that, as I have said, we have been working for many years to normalise the pension situation of politicians, and that is not because it is about a great deal of money in the large state accounts, but because it is about principles. When you insist on maintaining unfair privileges, while ordinary Danes are affected by reforms, by early retirement reforms, you send a completely wrong signal, as we see it, that we in here are worth more and have the right to more than ordinary Danes who go to work and do their jobs. That is why I can promise, I believe, that this will not be the last time that we in the Folketing will have to deal with our pension situation. Because history has actually shown that persistent pressure has meant that, step by step, we are getting small steps in the right direction and having limited privileges. So I guess we're not even going to the finish line? But thank you for the fight for this time.

ParlaMint-DK_20170516144617	I regret the interruption, but as the Minister knows, it is not possible to negotiate in the Folketing when the Minister is not present. But now the Minister is here. The debate is open. Here you go, Minister of Business.
ParlaMint-DK_20170516144643	I would like to start by regretting to the President and the Folketing that I was late for the proposal. Because, of course, we have to be there on time for proposals to be discussed in the Parliamentary Assembly. The proposal put forward here is, after all, the establishment of a mortgage institution for loans to owner-occupied housing and commercial property in peripheral areas. It is a re-submission of a motion for a resolution from the last Assembly, which was also tabled in the previous Assembly, and that is how we can go on and on. But it is fair and relevant to have the debate. We in the government believe that there are a number of problems with the proposal and therefore do not think that a State mortgage institution should be set up. Firstly, a State-supported mortgage institution, whether it be for deficit cover or interest rate subsidies, will require that an annual framework be set for it by the Finance Act, and that requires funding. Resources will also have to be allocated to the rating of borrowers, as such competencies are not present in the public system today. Secondly, the establishment of such a State institution could lead to a quota system in which only a limited proportion of potential borrowers in the outermost regions could obtain a loan. Thirdly, a government credit institution will address borrowers that private institutions will not serve because the value of the mortgage is deemed to be too uncertain. This will give the institution a loan portfolio with significant loss risks exceeding the loss risks of private institutions. In concrete terms, this means that the State will take on significant loss risks. Fourthly, it is the Government's immediate assessment that the proposed scheme would be in breach of the State aid rules. In conclusion, the government generally considers that the State should not intervene in private markets unless there are special circumstances to justify this. It should also be noted that a well-functioning credit market will result in both acceptance and refusal of loan requests. The refusal of credit requests therefore does not necessarily mean that the market is not functioning properly. So, overall, the government does not believe that state aid is the right way to go on this matter, and the government cannot therefore support the resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20170516152336	As the rapporteurs of the Danish People's Party and the Social Democratic Party have also said, I note that this is a motion for a resolution which we have dealt with several times here in the Danish Parliament. In the Left, we have been opposed to this in the past, and we have continued to do so, so let me just outline a few of the reasons for this. Basically, we do not think it is a state task to be a mortgage provider. The State will thus risk having to act as a company for those who cannot obtain loans under normal conditions and there is a high risk that they will be persons where the economy is assessed not to be suitable for receiving mortgage loans. It will then mean that it will be a company with a high risk of not being profitable, and the SF itself has come up with that recognition, in that it talks about a state guarantee, subsidy or otherwise a company to which the State and thus taxpayers have to pay. It will also require the State to set up credit rating systems that the State does not have at present, because it is basically the private market today that is making sure that people can get one or the other loan. And then there is probably a relatively obvious risk that this would be contrary to the EU's State aid rules in this area. Does this mean that this is not something we take seriously in the Left? No, it certainly does not, and that is why it was precisely one of the themes on which we were very concerned while we were in opposition. And, like so many other areas, we are going to think the same thing when we sit in government. Even when the report on funding in our rural areas came a year and a half ago, the then Left Government broke up with the six-month rule and the rules on availability were also relaxed. Basically, the best way to ensure the possibility of obtaining a loan also in our rural areas is for the economy to start in our rural areas so that jobs and prosperity are created and there is faith in the future. The fact that money can be made from borrowing money is, in fact, the best way to support development, and one can safely say that is something that the two governments that have been since 2015, that is, the current government and then the pure Left Government right after the last parliamentary elections, have taken care of. A number of initiatives have been taken to ensure a Denmark in better balance and growth and development throughout Denmark. I can mention the relocation of state jobs, increased efforts for tourism, and we are liberalising the planning act. We also abolish the PSO tax, which has affected many production companies located in our rural areas, and the list is much longer. I believe that over 120 specific initiatives have been carried out over the last two years, and it is precisely because we have the political ambition to create a better balance in Denmark and that there must be ample opportunity to work and live in all parts of Denmark. This is and remains the best condition for companies to invest and lend to people so that they can settle. Therefore, there is a long cool move to ensure a Denmark in better balance, and it will also help to support the possibility of financing its accommodation cheaply. For many, this will be through the mortgage credit system, and it may also be by other means. It is beneficial that there is a broad market with different offers, and I therefore believe that it is not beneficial for the state to interfere in that market. The only involvement of the state must be that the economy is in progress, and we can see that the rural statement that we discussed the other day shows that there are very many positive stories to tell about our rural areas and that development has turned. I think we should be happy about that, and perhaps we should be better at talking it up instead of talking down so much of Denmark and thus getting people to worry instead of saying: there are plenty of good stories and progress out there. It also helps to ensure that lending opportunities are improved. That is why we in the Left cannot support the SF motion for a resolution, and I have promised to welcome the Conservatives and say that they cannot support the proposal either.

ParlaMint-DK_20170516164426	The debate is open. The first to speak is the Minister for Industry. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20170516164447	Thank you. The proposers have proposed two specific points: that the Act on Restaurants should be amended so that the authorising authority should place emphasis on the decisions of the Equal Treatment Board when examining applications for funding and that the level of compensation for night-time discrimination should be significantly increased. In Denmark, discrimination has already been legislated on, and that is why we in the government believe that the existing rules and remedies are sufficient. Legislation on discrimination in relation to this issue is contained in the Law on the Prohibition of Discrimination on grounds of race, etc., which is the responsibility of the Ministry of Justice, and in the Law on Ethnic Equality, both of which contain a prohibition on discrimination on grounds of race, ethnic origin, belief, etc. Under both laws, you can complain if you experience discrimination. In addition, the Equal Opportunities Act prohibits traders from discrimination on grounds of sex, and the proposed change could be seen as very intrusive to the restaurateurs and could potentially have financial consequences for the grant holders. Finally, it is already possible today to refuse a restaurateur an authorization for alcohol and to renew an authorization for alcohol because of a judgment on discrimination. Under certain circumstances, an alcoholic authorization may also be dismissed by a court of law. Both the courts and the Equal Treatment Board deal with cases of discrimination. When it comes to the level of compensation, UIM is responsible for the law on ethnic equality, and it is the Equal Treatment Board that measures and grants compensation to victims of illegal discrimination. That is why I will refrain from commenting on this part of the proposal. The government believes that dialogue with the catering industry is the way forward and therefore cannot support the resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20170516171238	Thank you. The motion for a resolution by the Danish People's Party requires the government to take the necessary initiatives to strengthen the enforcement of intellectual property rights, IP rights, on the Internet. It follows from the proposal that action against infringements of intellectual property rights, inter alia: in the form of illegal file sharing, should be sharpened. In addition, the possibility of creating a special task force specialising in combating infringements of intellectual property rights on the Internet needs to be explored. First of all, let me state that the government fully agrees with the intentions behind the resolution. We in Denmark must have a strong focus on dealing with cases of infringement of intellectual property rights on the Internet, and we must keep track of developments and take the necessary initiatives in this area. This is also the reason why the Attorney General, together with SØIK and the National Police, will carry out an evaluation of initiatives already taken to strengthen the enforcement of intellectual property rights in 2017. We in the government have been concerned about how the comments on the proposal should be understood precisely. Overall, it is therefore some of the comments on the proposal that appear to require changes to concepts such as jurisdiction and liability subject, which are fundamental elements of the Danish criminal justice system. These parts of the penal system do not believe that we should change. We have therefore had a very good dialogue with the proposers of the Danish People's Party, also with the Social Democrats, on the motion for a resolution and on what we really want to do in this area. I am very pleased that the government, together with the Danish People's Party and the Social Democratic Party, has agreed on a joint statement which clarifies what the resolution actually means. The joint opinion will be part of the further work of the committee on the resolution, but I would just like to take this opportunity to read from the start of the discussion what we are agreed on. It is • and I quote: a majority (government, DF and S) is recommending the adoption of the resolution, with the majority emphasising that the basic principles of Danish criminal law require, among other things, that: Danish jurisdiction and responsibility subject, which can be prosecuted by the Danish authorities, is maintained. The following elements shall be included in the evaluation of the area in 2017: experience from other countries, including Great Britain and Sweden, SØIK's experience regarding international cooperation, as well as experience regarding legal barriers to tackling digital crime, how violations on foreign websites are handled today, rights holders' experience in enforcement. The right-holders should also be asked to contribute to the qualification of the extent of the problem of criminality, including possible links with other forms of crime. The studies shall result in a concrete recommendation as to whether a task force will contribute to the strengthening of enforcement of ipr. That recommendation shall be made in the first half of 2018. The evaluation shall indicate when the area is to be re-evaluated and the possibility of blocking websites based on financial crime shall be set out in the evaluation. Quote out. As I said at the outset, it is important that effective enforcement is carried out in the event of IPR violations. We in Denmark must have a strong focus on dealing with cases of infringement of intellectual property rights online, and we must continuously monitor developments and take the necessary initiatives in this area. I am very pleased that the proposers and the Social Democrats have helped to find a good solution in relation to the concrete form of the resolution, which responds both to the need for effective enforcement in the event of infringements and, at the same time, respects the fundamental principles of Danish criminal law. With the comments that follow from the joint opinion, I can say that the government can support the resolution and I look forward to the further consideration of the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20170516171820	Thank you to the authors of the motion for a resolution for raising a very important debate. If you were not already aware that this is a problem, at least it was fully established at the recent hearing. The fight against violations of rights on the Internet is a very important issue and, above all, because technology has developed rapidly in recent years and legislation has not necessarily followed suit. This is not only true when we talk about identity theft, data fraud and revenge porn. This also applies when we talk about violations of intellectual property rights on the Internet, and thus about the protection of art, images, literature and design. Back in 2013, we discussed a similar motion for a resolution. At that time, a number of concrete initiatives had been taken to strengthen efforts in this area by means of a special effort both in SØIK and in the individual police circles. However, as I said before, developments in this area are extremely strong, at a furious pace, and therefore we must always do what we can on the part of the Danish Parliament to keep up with them. The Social Democrats therefore agree with the proposers that our legal system is basically tailored to a world without the Internet, and that therefore there is a need for us to adapt, at an even higher rate, the possibilities of preventing, investigating and, above all, prosecuting the violations taking place in the digital world and, above all, putting an end to the blatant violations that are taking place. That is why we must ensure that the police and prosecutors have the right tools to solve and prosecute these cases. In addition, copyright, including copyright management, is incredibly important for Danish artists and Danish cultural content and production. If they are not protected from violations of their rights, they simply lose their livelihood, and that does not mean that we do not get the art and culture that we want so much and that is so important a part of our society. That is why we believe that we must also ensure that digital rights are protected, because this is precisely the basis of Danish art and culture. The Minister explained so well what an agreement we have made on this matter in order to move one step further. That is why I shall not go into it further. I would just like to say that we look forward to discussing this further during the work of the committee and to supporting the statement that the Minister of Justice read out. In those words, we also support the motion for a resolution here.

ParlaMint-DK_20170516175707	The debate is open and I shall begin by giving the floor to the Minister for Justice.
ParlaMint-DK_20170516175728	Thank you. The motion for a resolution tabled by the Danish People's Party aims to maintain an effective reporting system while at the same time protecting the staff of the criminal services against reprisals from the persons being reported. The Government fully agrees with these objectives. Nor is there any doubt that the anonymisation of alerts in connection with access to documents can help protect staff and thus also ensure that staff do not fail to report for fear of reprisals. The reality is, however, that criminal services already have the opportunity today to anonymise reports when needed, among other things. where necessary to prevent crime. This means, in practice, that there is wide access to anonymise concerns reports in the interests of the security of prison officers. However, there will also be situations where there is no risk to disclose the identity of the notifier. In that case, there is no reason to anonymise the report. It is a fundamental principle in Denmark that you as a citizen can obtain information about the cases dealt with by the authorities. This also applies to information on who has been involved in the proceedings. Access to such information may be crucial to the citizen's ability to defend his or her interests. It can, for example, be done. allow misunderstandings or inability to be identified by the case officers, and therefore no anonymisation should be carried out without any reason. Since the Department of Justice already has the opportunity today to anonymise reports when necessary, there is no need to change the rules. In addition, the resolution we have today will not solve the challenges of inadequate anonymisation that can arise in practice. I am thinking here, for example. the situation that has been mentioned in the past. Here a teacher's name was anonymized, but not the title, and since the inmate had only one teacher, it was easy to figure out who the reviewer was. This was a clear mistake and this kind of error will not prevent the resolution. It was a human error. The possibility of anonymising alerts already includes all identifiable information. I do not believe that a general rule on anonymisation will be a guarantee that anonymisation is actually effective. Against this background, the government does not consider that a general rule on the anonymisation of concerns reports is the right way to go. Of course, in relation to a reporting system, it is important that the criminal services staff are safe in reporting. We must therefore ensure that they actually make use of the possibility of anonymisation when needed and, of course, ensure that anonymisation is effective and actually hides the identity of the notifier. In January of this year, the Ministry of Justice sent a letter to the Department of Crime with an indication of the possibilities of anonymisation. The Ministry of Justice is also working on a guide to the exchange of information on radicalisation by public authorities. We would also like to draw attention to the possibility of anonymising reports. I am very concerned to ensure proper working conditions for the prison officers. Of course, they must be able to do their job without being subjected to violence, threats or harassment by the inmates. Since last summer, a number of initiatives have been taken to improve the safety of prison officers more generally. At the same time, the government is concerned that we do not derogate from general guarantees of legal certainty when there is no reason to do so. Although the government fully agrees with the objectives of having an effective reporting system and at the same time protecting the staff of criminal services, we cannot support this proposal.

This can be seen from a reply from the Ministry of Justice to the Legal Affairs Committee in January 2017. Here the Ministry writes among other things. The right of Insight under Article 31 of the Personal Data Act may include: limited if a data subject's interest in obtaining information is found to give way to essential considerations of private or public interest, including for reasons of State security or to prevent crime. As the Ministry writes in the same letter, the law in practice ensures that the criminal services will be able to largely refrain from providing information on concerns reports or to anonymise the reports. That is why we consider this motion for a resolution superfluous in the alternative and cannot therefore vote in favour of it. What we want with the proposal is this automatic anonymisation of everything, and we do not think that is the right way to go. We believe it is important to be able, of course, to anonymise when necessary, but at the same time it is also an important principle for us that we have an open and transparent legal system that uses anonymisation only when it is considered absolutely necessary.

ParlaMint-DK_20170516190112	Thank you to the rapporteur of the Left. The next speaker is Mr. Rune Lund as rapporteur for the Enhedslisten.
ParlaMint-DK_20170516190134	From the point of view of the Enhedslisten, we cannot support this proposal as a whole. However, we think that the first part of the proposal has a lot of sensible thought in it, and that is really what I hear from several other rapporteurs, that is when we talk about a particular legal path for young people. From our point of view, we think it is important to have a specific legal trail for young people, we think this is a very good idea, and we think it is a good thing to have to discuss it, and we look forward to discussing it in the context of the forthcoming negotiations. So we can support the intention behind and the ideas of that element, namely that there is a need to discuss something more, how we can use restorative justice, to restore justice, to children and to young people outside the general system of justice. Those thoughts are clear Unity Tankage, and that is a good way to have an approach. However, we cannot support reducing the age of criminals, and we cannot support reducing them to 14, 13 or 12 years. That is why we will not be voting in favour of this motion for a resolution. If you look at the statistics and what the group of young people who commit crime is doing from crime, it is typically such things as theft, vandalism and theft of use. To put it a little harshly, when children commit crime, it is because they steal candy from the grocery store, graffiti from the neighbor or steal a bike to drive home on. Of course you can't. When there are children who do such a thing, they must be watched and care must be taken to see what problems lie behind them and that this is not something that turns into a criminal career. This is where the primary school has already to look at the worrying behaviour that some children may have and look at the cooperation between the police and the municipality. For example: You can look at how to solve some of these problems and intervene early with preventive action. If you choose to punish children through a criminal justice system, it can have very violent consequences, among others. because the criminal justice system is not geared at all to dealing with children, but also because they are given a stained criminal record and therefore worse prospects for the future. We must not neglect the stigmatisation that may lie in it. In terms of reducing the criminal low age, it has been attempted under the former bourgeois government of the VK. It was reduced to 14 years in 2009, and this did not change the pattern of crime among 14-year-olds. Here the police could also tell it was East Jutland Police that the only change they experienced was that now they had to spend much more time dealing with small cases and rapes, etc., because this is usually what children make when they violate the law. The criminal low age was then fortunately set up again when a new government came to power in 2011. After all, children do not necessarily think about the consequences of their actions in the way that adults do before the trap claps. They have not yet learned that, and therefore a lower criminal low age will not stop them in any action they may take. For children are not so calculating and so foresightful. They have not yet learned to think about the way adults think, and thus they do not think about the consequences of their actions. That is why, at best, a lowered criminal shallow age will be powerless and, as far as possible, it can be harmful at worst. What is our goal is to keep young people out of a criminal race. However, children do not have a maturity and development which means that they are similarly able to predict the consequences of their actions and operate in a criminal justice system. That is precisely why we are sending them into a strong social system, because, of course, breaking the law should not be a consequence. There must be a strong social hand teaching young people who have crossed the border how to behave like a responsible person. But if you send children into a criminal justice system instead, you risk teaching them, not how you are responsible, as you should be, but how you are a criminal. There is therefore a risk of stamping children and aggravating the situation. Therefore, the proposal to lower the criminal low age symbol of the worst drawer, which will at best be powerless, but which is likely to be seriously harmful. ParlaMint-DK_20170516194239	About two years ago, the party chairmen of the four then opposition parties wrote a common chronicle in Berlingske. In the cliché they outlined the framework for a new model that, in the past and with greater focus on clear consequences, should help to put even better emphasis on juvenile delinquency. Here, at least, the clear impression was given that when the chroniclers were given power, as they had observed, they would reduce the criminal low age and set up juvenile courts. That majority is now a reality. Blue block, the so-called blue block, won the election. A resolution was also tabled on which the four then and present party leaders in the blue bloc had agreed. The substance of the motion for a resolution is essentially what we are discussing, namely the reduction of the age of criminals and the establishment of youth courts. Now we are almost two years into the new parliamentary term, two years in which the so-called blue bloc has had a majority, and there is still no real news on this very important issue, which was a must for the blue before the elections and must therefore also be for them now. That is a pity, and that is why we in the Danish People's Party have taken the baton, which is why we are today debating this motion for a resolution. The system we have today needs changes. We need a whole system that embraces young people, some of them very young people, who have had a crooked start in life, a life that leads to crime from the beginning of life. This can be due to many things: bad friends, a family that cares nothing about them, yes, many things. But what must not go wrong is the opportunity that we must offer to the young person. For all young people must have a good and fair chance in life. We owe them that, but equally we owe it to the victims it will suffer if and when young people commit crime. Today we maintain a very square limit, the so-called criminal low age of 15 years, with one system for them under 15 years and another system for them above, one type of sanctions for them under and another for them over. That is why we have rethinked that system. Why not have a system for all young people specialising in young people, their opportunities and their needs; a system that will also increase legal certainty for young people; a system that can take action on several different fronts to help young people out of crime before they grow into it? For nothing is more sad than wasted life. That is why we in the Danish People's Party believe the same as we did at the time of 2014 when we discussed the previous motion for a resolution. Many things have been said and written about the proposal. To put it bluntly, many wrong and misleading things have been said about our proposal. I assume that it is conscious and in the absence of better arguments, but now allow me to address a few of them so that we can hopefully avoid spending time on them in the further debate. It is regularly said that with the proposal here we will put children in prison. To that I can just say: No, children are not going to jail, of course. Children and young people must have a maximum of secure institutions with educational staff, and the motion for a resolution here does not change anything about that. It is said that it is all about punishment, but it is not a punishment that we are taking care of a biased development. On the contrary, it helps. For me it's about a lot of things; it's about a lot of things. to have the right sanction against a particular behaviour. By creating a system in which young people, who for the first time cross the line, are directed by • and this can happen with further sanctions • then we avoid both victims later on and that the person later in life becomes hardened criminal. So no, it's not about punishment. It's about making the toolbox bigger, it's about solving the problems that are. Then there is the claim that we will brand very young people with a criminal record. And with regard to the word brand, I would like to thank Mrs Trine Bramsen in particular for that language flower. But no, instead of making an actual criminal record, Dansk Folkeparti proposes a youth certificate so that young people can seek a relevant job if it is compatible with the sanction they have been given. In order to increase motivation, the content of the certificate can be deleted if the young person demonstrates a real ability to act, a desire to go a clearly more viable way in life. Finally, there is the claim that young people cannot understand consequences. With this approach, we are talking down directly to young people, because of course young people understand the consequences. There's just so many options in this field. There's a huge potential to change this system, so I think we should get started on it, preferably as soon as possible. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20170516195226	Thank you for the explanatory statement. Then I give the floor to the Minister of Justice for answers.
ParlaMint-DK_20170516195235	Thank you. At any rate, I would like to begin by stating that I strongly reject persons or groups who are trying to promote their positions by violent means or other forms of criminal activity. This also applies, of course, if those concerned try to prevent others from exercising their right to demonstrate or express themselves peacefully. Unfortunately, the unrest we saw during the 10th anniversary of the clearing of the former youth house at Jagtvej was a sad reminder that there are people in Denmark who are prepared to use violent means to advance their own political agenda. It makes me sad, and it makes me angry. I can guarantee that this government will do everything in its power to combat violent extremism, whether it is manifesting itself on the political left or on the right. Before going into the government's initiatives in this area, I would like briefly to explain the police and PET's action against violent demonstrators and violent extremism. In this context, I would also like to refer to the police's efforts in connection with the 10th anniversary of the clearing of the former youth house. The National Police have reported that a police operation generally includes three main phases: a planning phase, a phase of action and an evaluation phase. The planning phase is a description of all preparatory actions undertaken prior to events known to the police. At this stage, intelligence is being collected, analysed and a threat assessment is being carried out. At the same time, strategic objectives will be set for action. The necessary resources are requested and cooperation is established with external stakeholders and, where possible, a dialogue with e.g. well-known demonstration leaders. Precisely an initial dialogue with demonstration leaders is essential, because the police have a duty to protect the right of citizens to gather together. This is also the reason why demonstrations must be reported to the police so that the police have the opportunity to redirect the movement and so that any counter-demonstration can ensure that both groups can exercise their right to freedom of assembly and freedom of expression. After the planning phase comes the action phase, which includes the actual police action. Every effort is based on the fact that the police must use as little physical force as possible in their efforts. In relation to demonstrations considered to be a threat to public peace, the police can use a number of means of both physical and non-physical nature. It depends on the actual situation, which means are being used. There can be, among other things: be mandatory, individual arrests or dissolution of the demonstration. To manage the effort, an effort management is established, including: present at street level. During the evaluation phase, the police look at learning points from concrete police action. The purpose of the evaluation phase is to continuously improve police action in similar situations. In connection with the 10th anniversary of the clearance of the former youth house at Jagtvej on 1 March 2017, the Copenhagen Police have informed that the police received a notification of the demonstration in January 2017. The notifier indicated that it was expected that approx. 260 people would participate in a demonstration on the occasion of the 10th anniversary and that tabs and a speaker car would be used. The Copenhagen Police had already begun planning their action one month before receiving the notification on the basis of a clear expectation that a demonstration would be held during the 10th anniversary of the grubbing-up. It was the police and PET's assessment that 300-400 participants could be expected in the demonstration. During the days prior to the demonstration, the head of effort at Copenhagen Police was in dialogue with the protest manager and had agreed in this connection that the demonstration should take its starting point from Mrs. Plads instead of Gammel Torv as a result of extensive road work on the site. The Copenhagen Police took the view that the dialogue was good and constructive, which supported the overall intelligence picture and the expectation that the demonstration would take place in good order. On the day of the demonstration there was an ongoing influx of participants for the demonstration. About 2:00 a.m. 17.00 the number of participants up to 800-900 was therefore significantly more than the number reported and significantly more than the police had estimated. Many of the participants were families who brought prams and bicycles. At the beginning of the demonstration there was a small group of approx. 20 masked people who took the lead in the demonstration procession. They threw bottles in the direction of police traffic officers who were responsible for directing the demonstration through traffic. The demonstration then continued along the planned route, and shortly before Queen Louise's Bridge, bottles and stones were thrown again against the police. In view of the escalating unrest and the number of demonstrators, the Copenhagen Police decided to step up their efforts, including: by requesting additional resources from the National Police Department. The demonstration then continued on Nørrebrogade, where the riots developed further, with vandalism being carried out against several banks and ATMs. The police chief was continuously in dialogue with the protest leader, who said that he could not control the many troublemakers. At this time the Copenhagen Police estimated that at least 200 of the participants were involved in the serious disturbances of public order. When the widespread riots came out of the demonstration and it was considered to be a danger to public peace, the assembly was disbanded. The formal catch form used for the dissolution of an assembly was communicated to protesters several times via loudspeaker cars and megaphones. When the assembly was dissolved, several of the peaceful participants left the demonstration, whereas the masked protesters moved to the site at Hungtvej 69, where they lit fires and built barricades. By this time the police were very numerous. In order to stop the continuing escalating riots, protestors were pushed out on Nørrebrogade and followed to the Youth House on Dortheavej. A few arrests were made along the way. On arrival at Dortheavej, many of the protestors entered the Youth House and several went outside. The protesters in the youth house were masked, aggressive, and agitated and threw various kinds of shooters against the police, including bottles, stones, pétanque balls, paint bombs, spears and other forms of made weapons. In order to stop the riots and to ensure the possibility of prosecuting the perpetrators, the Copenhagen Police decided to search the Youth House and register the persons present. During the planning of the action, the demonstrators chose voluntarily to leave the Youth House, presumably as a result of the police's continued dialogue with the protest leader. In this connection, the protesters were registered and expelled. On the ground, it was not possible to link certain demonstrators to criminal offences. The Copenhagen Police then searched the youth house and found caste shooters, painting bombs, pétanque bullets, a prepared spear, cobblestone, etc. The police were subsequently massively present in Copenhagen the evening and the night, and no further unrest or disorder was detected. As part of the effort, seven people were arrested and two minors were detained. I would like to pay tribute to the police for their efforts on that day. What we witnessed in connection with the riots of 1 March was a professional police who handled a difficult situation excellent and exemplary. I have also noted that the police have recently announced that they will target a large number of people in the wake of the riots. Now we have to see how the specific cases are proceeding. However, I would say that the respect package of last year for me was a clear signal from the government that there must be respect for our police and other public servants. The package includes: a punishment for all forms of violence and threats to the police in the context of riots, as well as a new increase in liability. From police action against violent demonstrators, I would like to go on to say something about the task of police intelligence, because it is and must be a central task for PET to monitor and to try to prevent extremists on the extreme right or left, for example, from using extreme or undemocratic methods. This is particularly true if extremists, with violence or other criminal offences, try to prevent the right of other persons to make their views known. Unfortunately, according to PET, there are political extremist environments in Denmark that are prepared to use violence to highlight their own agenda. The extremist violence is being corrected, among other things. against political opponents as well as organisations and companies that are given symbolic value. In this context, PET is paying close attention to, inter alia: the left-wing extremist environment in Denmark. This is obviously the case when the environment organises counter-demonstrations against, for example, For Freedom demonstrations. PET also cooperates closely with the other police in order to protect citizens' right to assemble. And it is a task that is taken very seriously. What, then, are we doing from a political point of view to prevent and combat extremism? In Denmark, we have been focusing for many years on preventing and combating extremism in all forms, including violent left-wing extremism. Changing governments have since 2009 drawn up action plans that provide the authorities with the best possible conditions for preventing and combating extremism. No later than October last year, the previous government published a new action plan, including: will give PET a better opportunity to get a clear picture of how extremist propaganda on the Internet and social media can contribute to radicalisation and recruitment. With the measure, PET gets better tools to understand what affects young people to be recruited into extremist or radicalised environments. With that knowledge base it will be easier for the police and PET to close the taps to the extremist environments. We must safeguard freedom of expression and assembly as fundamental values in our society, but there are, of course, limits on how to behave, even when you are part of an assembly. What we saw on the 10th anniversary of the grubbing-up of the former youth house was an example of an entirely unacceptable overrun of all the limits of freedom of assembly that apply to all of us. As Minister of Justice, it makes me sad to see such behavior, and it makes me angry. We should never accept such behavior. We're gonna get rid of that. Significant efforts are already being made by the police and PET to combat violent extremist environments, and from the political point of view we have launched an action plan that will give PET better tools in the fight against extremism and radicalisation. I am very pleased with the efforts being made today, but if there is a need to intensify the efforts of the authorities in this area, I am, of course, prepared to discuss it.

ParlaMint-DK_20170516203308	The debate is open. Minister of Justice, please.
ParlaMint-DK_20170516203329	The 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development and its 17 World Goals, together with the COP 21 Global Climate Agreement in Paris, constitute the global vision for sustainable development in the world. It is the government's clear ambition that Denmark, both internationally and nationally, should help to realise this vision of sustainable development in the world. Therefore, the follow-up to the UN's global goals and the climate agreement are at the heart of the government's development policy and climate policy, and that is also why the government launched an action plan for Denmark's follow-up to the World Goals in March. On this point, we are fully in line with the proposers. Today, however, the key issue is the drafting of legislative proposals rather than the 17 objectives of the world. With the motion for a resolution, the proposers call on the government to introduce a system which will involve mandatory impact assessments of legislative proposals in order to clarify the positive or negative impact of the bill on the Danish contribution to the achievement of the UN World Sustainable Development Goals and the Paris Agreement's climate objectives. Today, it is the case that legislative proposals should include a review of, among other things. economic consequences as well as environmental and gender impact when they significantly affect a given area, and I welcome the proposers to inform them that the government has, in this way, already decided to go further. Earlier this year, the government launched an action plan for Denmark's follow-up to the UN's Global Sustainable Development Goal. As indicated in the action plan, the government wishes to see legislative proposals and other actions assessed in the future in relation to the UN's global sustainable development goals, as appropriate, and if the consequences of these matters are significant. The motion for a resolution we are debating today means that consequences for the sustainability objectives and climate consequences must always be mentioned in the individual legislative proposals, regardless of whether this is relevant and significant in relation to the specific legislative proposal. The Government does not believe that such assessments should be a separate mandatory point in the comments of the draft legislative proposals, which the motion for a resolution proposes, and in this respect it is important for me to give priority to the following: the guidance given by the Ministry of Justice on the quality of legislation contains rules that the general comments of the draft legislation should include some mandatory points on certain consequences of the draft law. The mandatory element is that the points must be included, even if the draft law does not have the actual consequences that the paragraph deals with. The existing mandatory items have been selected on the basis of an assessment of which issues are generally most relevant for the assessment of legislative proposals. This is a balancing exercise in order to ensure that the comments are thorough and complete in view of the fact that the comments should only refer to relevant issues. There are now nine mandatory points in the legislative notes. One of these points is the point of environmental impact, which includes both environmental and climate impact. In addition, the Guide on Quality of Law also states that, where appropriate, there may be grounds for explaining the consequences of a legislative proposal other than those set out in the mandatory paragraphs. It can, for example, be done. • the impact of the draft law on regional policy or on gender equality. Several of the objectives set for the UN's global goals will therefore already be covered by the points mentioned in the draft law. As regards the climate effects of a legislative proposal, there is a hang-up to such an assessment in the environmental impact assessment in the draft law. In addition, as mentioned earlier, the government has already decided to go further to follow up the achievement of the UN's global goals. Making the issue of impact on the sustainability objectives and climate targets mandatory will entail a new administrative burden for ministries. In addition, I would also point out that, from a purely legal point of view, it is a quality in itself that the comments on a legislative proposal are limited to a reference to the issues that are relevant in that context. In fact, this restriction helps to ensure that the issues relevant to be taken into account in the legislative proposal in question are kept in focus. In this regard, I also note that no matter how important it is to be described if a legislative proposal has implications for Denmark's achievement of the United Nations World Goals and the Paris Agreement's climate objectives, it will be far from all the proposals we are dealing with in this Chamber that actually have such consequences. If a new mandatory paragraph is introduced, which deals only with the impact of the bill on the United Nations World Goals and the climate objectives of the Paris Agreement, it will raise questions as to which other cross-cutting issues should also be included as separate points in the comments to a legislative proposal. The inclusion of a new separate compulsory item could, in time, have the unintended effect of losing the clarity which the fixed points would have to contribute to creating. The government does not want to contribute to this. Against this background, the Government cannot support the motion for a resolution to introduce a new mandatory paragraph.

ParlaMint-DK_20170516205727	I would just like to mention this in the motion for a resolution to remind you of what we are discussing here: making it compulsory for all legislative proposals dealt with in the Folketing to be assessed in terms of how they meet the UN's 17 world objectives and the Paris climate agreement. It is the case that there are a number of things that are now compulsory to assess, among other things. how legislative proposals have an impact on economic and administrative consequences for the state, regions and municipalities, and accordingly for business, the impact on the environment, EU law and administrative consequences for citizens. These are the things that are now mandatory, and then there is also the possibility today that impact assessments can be carried out in all kinds of other areas where it may be relevant to the subject covered by the draft law. I would like to say that we in the Danish People's Party believe that this is fully adequate and adequate for the need. We are surprised that the proposers are making the proposal here. We are aware that the UN's global objective is also something that Denmark has agreed to, the same with the Paris climate agreement, and we are fully aware of that, but to elevate the UN's global goals and the Paris climate agreement to such a special sacred status that everything must be measured up, we believe that is going too far. This can be done where appropriate. I simply do not see when we are making social policy why it should then be assessed against the Paris climate agreement and I could give several other examples. We also fear, if the motion for a resolution is adopted here, that it will then be used as a lever to ensure that the proposers will certainly come up with other resolutions in which other things should be elevated to a special status, for example. the Convention on Human Rights, the Convention on Refugees and several other things that we in the Danish People's Party do not consider appropriate to interfere with Danish legislation and which we do not think should be given special status. So in these words, we in the Danish People's Party reject the motion for a resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20170516220142	The debate is open and I give the floor to the Minister for Children and Social Affairs.
ParlaMint-DK_20170516220210	Thank you. The alternative and Enhedslisten have tabled motion for a resolution No. B 119, which obliges the government to propose before the end of 2017 a law amending the relevant rules of the Children's Law and amending the practice of defining the gender of parents on the basis of the legal and hence on the basis of the self-chosen sex. The motion for a resolution emphasises that there are parts of Danish legislation which do not respect and accommodate the choices of trans-nationals. One example, according to the proposers, is the rules of the Children's Law on Parenthood. Let me start by saying that this is a relevant issue raised by the proposers. I would also like to point out that a person's desire and ability to become a parent are not necessarily influenced by the fact that the person has made a legal change of sex. The legal gender change legislation is based on a declaration model. The model implies that transpersonals can obtain legal change of sex without requiring surgical or other gender-corrective treatment. This means that a person who has received a legal change of sex will continue to have his biological sex and that the biological sex can be used in accordance with the wishes and preferences of the person concerned for example to have children. In addition, it is also possible for a legal gender change person to use assisted reproduction, including a donor, if the person cannot or does not want to use his own biological germ cells. Thus, there are more opportunities to become a parent, even after a legal change of sex. For me, it is important that the legislation supports the many different family forms we have in Denmark, and that also applies to who is a child's parents. The primary purpose of the Children's Act is to establish legal parenthood, including the determination of a fatherhood or a parenthood of a child. In addition, the children's law is based on the basic Roman legal principle of mater est, which provides that the woman who has given birth to a child is to be regarded as the mother of the child. And in law it is directly regulated that the principle also applies when assisted reproduction is used. In its structure, the Children's Law is based on the use of both a sperm cell and an egg to make a child, even when assisted reproduction is used. And the sex used to propagate is thus decisive for legal parenthood in the law. The replacement of a donor or the subsequent change of the legal sex of a parent does not matter. And thus, the children's law allows a man or a woman who does not give birth to a child to become a parent as a parent and as a mother. Therefore, the rules of the Children's Act do not prevent a person who has carried out a legal change of sex from becoming a parent, regardless of whether the person uses his or her own germ cells or donated germ cells. The children's law is designed in such a way that the rights contained in the law include: is derived from the sex of the person concerned, that is, the sex of which they have been used to propagate, and this gives a firm legal basis. As an example, the mother can raise a case of fatherhood and co-motherhood before the child is born, that the mother has a duty to inform who is or may be the father or co-mother of the child, and that a man who has had a sexual relationship with the mother has the right, as a starting point, to know whether he is the child's father. Before the Danish Parliament adopted the law allowing for a legal change of sex, an inter-ministerial working group had been set up to deal with the importance of a legal change of sex to the provisions of the Children's Act on paternity, maternity and co-maternity. There the working group recommended to maintain the starting point that the biological sex of the parents at the child's conception determines their designation in the sense of the Children's Act, and I have also recently explained this in a written reply to the Parliamentary Equality Committee. The recommendation of the Working Group has thus been followed and no changes have been made to the Children's Act as a consequence of the possibility of obtaining a legal change of sex. I have been informed that when a person applies for a legal change of sex, it is the CPR Office's procedure to acknowledge receipt of an application for a legal change of sex and at the same time to attach written guidance and information to the applicant, among others. The following can be seen: "Your legal status as a parent of a child under the Children's Act does not change even if you change gender. In the sense of the Children's Law, you have the sex that you use to propagate, and so it is the biological sex of the parents at the child's conception that determines their role in the children's law as either parent, mother or co-mother. A person who gives birth to a child, regardless of whether he is legally a man, will be regarded as the mother of the child within the meaning of the Children's Act. The authors of the motion find it a pity and imperfection that we do not go all the way and ensure that the rights follow in all parts of life in a slightly different way: if you have said A, you must also say B. However, it is now the case that a person who has made a legal change of sex has not gone all the way to changing sex. The gender has changed legally, while the biological sex remains unchanged. And it contains the children's law, just as the law contains other family patterns when it comes to determining who is the child's mother, father or co-mother. The children's law is based on the biological sex, which is still the focal point when making children. It may be a little traditional and probably a conservative approach in a modern world, but it provides a well-known and clear basis for who are the child's legal parents and what rights they have. And this supports the consideration behind the rules of the Children's Law, namely that the child has two legal parents. In addition, a legal gender changer has the opportunity to switch back to its original sex. If the legal sex becomes decisive, it could give rise to changes in legal parenthood with a lack of continuity for the child. I would like to make it clear once again that the legislation does not prevent a person who has had a legal change from becoming a parent. And basically, I believe that the legislation contains diversity and supports the variety of family forms that we have here in Denmark. At the same time, the current state of law gives the necessary predictability and legal basis, taking as its starting point the sex that can be used to have children, that is to say the biological sex. I have talked a lot about law, and I do, because the Children's Act lays down the legal community. But something quite different is what you call yourself at home and what kind of family you are. Today, children grow up in many different family constellations and manage to navigate them, and law often does not affect the child. On the other hand, it is the daily care and love of parents that determine the child's best interests, and parents are free to call themselves according to their legal sex, by their first name or by something quite different. We cannot and must not interfere. As has also been indicated by the Ministry in connection with the introduction of the possibility of obtaining a legal change of sex, the Ministry will monitor developments in this area and continuously assess the need for clarification or amendment of the Children's Act. Against this background, the government cannot support the motion for a resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20170516224016	Thank you. As a starting point, when the legal gender change was adopted in 2014, the Conservative People's Party was opposed, and that is why the Conservative People's Party is, of course, also opposed to the resolution here today. Before the Danish Parliament adopted the law allowing for legal gender change, an inter-ministerial working group had been set up in relation to the importance of legal gender change in the provisions of the Children's Act on paternity, maternity and citizenship. It was recommended by the working party to maintain the starting point that the biological sex of the parents at the child's conception constitutes their designation in the sense of the Children's Act. The recommendation of the working group was followed and therefore no amendments to the children's law should be made as a consequence of the possibility of obtaining legal gender change. In a case concerning a person seeking legal change of sex, it is the CPR Office's procedure to acknowledge receipt of an application for legal change of sex, while adding a written guide and information to applicants for the following: Your legal status as a father or mother of a child under child law does not change even if you change gender. In the sense of child law, you have the sex you use to procreate with. Thus, it is the biological sex of parents at the child's conception that determines their role in the child's law as either a parent or a co-mother. A person who gives birth to a child, regardless of whether he is legally a man, would be to be regarded as the mother of the child in the sense of the Children's Law. After all, we are in here to touch what is the whole foundation of our families. After all, we are going to touch the fact that the next one will probably be a proposal for neuter. I just have to say that the proposal here is so far-reaching that one can hardly understand it.
