ParlaMint-DK_20170516224920	The debate is open and I give the floor to the Minister for the Elderly.
ParlaMint-DK_20170516224943	Today we are debating a motion for a resolution on the introduction of more permanent teams in municipal home care. B 139 requires the government to submit legislative proposals for phasing in more permanent teams in municipal home care, so that the municipality of the individual home help recipient must assess the number of home help visits to be carried out by the citizen's permanent assistants. The aim of the proposal is, according to the proposers of the SF, that citizens should experience higher quality and coherence in municipal home care, and to ensure that, as far as possible, vulnerable elderly people are always visited by helpers from the same team. The proposers refer to a number of articles and to studies since 2014 on citizens' experience of quality in municipal home care. The articles indicate how important it is for citizens that employees who come to their homes are familiar faces and that the number of employees is reduced as far as possible. On the whole, I would like to start by saying that we must do everything we can to create the highest possible quality of care and care for the weakest elderly. There should be no doubt about that. I also fully recognise that the organisation of home care, including the influence of the individual citizen on help and the number of different helpers at home, is of great importance for the citizen's experience of quality in home care. The fewest possible different employees in the citizen's home will always be preferable. In my view, it should be a perfectly natural goal for both municipal and private suppliers of home care to have the individual citizen meet as few different employees as possible. Known faces create security for citizens who receive home care. And, of course, it is of great importance to the individual elderly citizen that there are fewest possible different employees in the home and that helpers know the habits and routines of the citizen and can find their way around the home. But for me there is such an absolute fact that it is and remains the individual municipal council that is responsible for organising and organising at all times how to solve the tasks of home care with high quality for the benefit and benefit of citizens in each municipality. Each municipal council is responsible for ensuring a good and appropriate organisation of home care, taking into account both the wishes of citizens and local needs. In this context, municipalities must ensure that there are no more different employees in the home than necessary and that the employees who come to the home have the necessary skills. We cannot and must not dictate it from Christiansborg. That would be to move at the essence of municipal self-government, the local responsibility, and that would be a governance in the detail that makes no sense. If we lay down in legislation rules on how municipalities should organise and organise home care, we are losing autonomy, and the government does not want that. In fact, I also find that for several years the municipalities have been focusing precisely on the organisation and organisation of home care, because the municipalities are aware of the importance for the citizens that the number of different employees entering the citizens' homes is as low as possible. That is clear, among other things. also by the 2013 report of the Home Aid Commission. For the purposes of examining the motion for a resolution, the Ministry has asked KL how the municipalities are working today to limit the number of different helpers in the home. KL states that many municipalities work to send the same employee as far as possible to the same citizen when planning the distribution of tasks. However, there are different conditions that will always have an impact on planning and the number of employees who come into a citizen's home. First of all, home care carries out a wide range of tasks, ranging from practical assistance such as cleaning to more complex care tasks such as: wound care. The different types of tasks require different competences of the employees, and therefore there may be different tasks for the individual citizen that will have to be solved by different employees. It can, for example, be done. be that a social and health worker is responsible for the practical tasks of a home, while the social and health assistant is responsible for the more complex care tasks. Secondly, unexpected situations may arise which require a change in the distribution of tasks and staff at short notice. An emergency illness may occur in a citizen, which means that an employee cannot reach his next visit and must ask a colleague to take over the visit. The quality of home care is precisely that of care and care, so that we do not leave a citizen who has an acute deterioration that requires action. Here, of course, there must be sufficient flexibility in planning for an employee to be able to stay with the citizen and another employee to be able to join the next citizen who should have had a visit. But not only the organisation and organisation of home care is of great importance to how many different employees come into the citizen's home. The general development in this area also makes more demands on home care than in the past. Many of the elderly, who today care for home care, have among other things: because of a positive development in health, where long hospital stays have been shortened, more complex care needs than we saw in the past. And this means that more elderly people in their own homes will often need more visits over the 24 hours. At the same time, a more complex disease picture among the elderly means that other and higher demands are made on the skills of the employees in home care. The municipalities need to take this into account, because the employees who come to the citizens' homes must have the skills that are necessary for this particular citizen. In other words, it is the government's view that the municipalities are already working on the issue that the proposal seeks to address with legislation. Let me conclude, therefore, by stating that we must constantly focus on how to ensure the greatest possible dignity and quality in caring for the weakest elderly. And the number of employees that citizens meet at home is of great importance for the citizens' experience of quality in home care. However, this does not change the fact that it is the responsibility of the municipalities to organise and organise home care so as to take as much account as possible of the citizens, also in relation to the number of helpers in the individual home. I do not think that setting targets in the legislation for how many employees should come into the individual home makes sense. It is clearly a municipal responsibility, whether the supplier is a local authority or a private supplier. We're not going to dictate this in this detail from Christiansborg. That is why the government cannot support the proposal.

So both for the safety of the elderly, but also to provide good care, it makes real sense to ensure that as far as possible some of the same home carers come. We know that there are municipalities today who are doing what they can to minimise the number of employees who come with the elderly, but we also know that sometimes it is difficult to make it work together. It may be economics, there is an explanation that one then does not get the work done in the municipality. There is also a problem that many employees in home care are part-time, although they actually often want to come full-time. It also makes it more difficult to plan that it is very much the same helpers who come. So we certainly think it is relevant to look at what one could do to make it more permanent teams that come from municipal home care. There are several of my fellow rapporteurs up here who say that this is contrary to municipal autonomy. In other words, one might wonder why, a few weeks ago, when we made a proposal which was that it should be up to the municipalities themselves to decide whether there should be free choice in home care, yes, the same parties thought that it could not be tampered with at all. One might say that it would also be to add it to municipal autonomy if it were to be a choice one could make for oneself, i.e. whether one would use private actors. In this context, however, it was not at all a question of strengthening local self-government, as it is now called. In the same way, we see that the same parties think that it is quite reasonable for Christiansborg to decide here that dignity policies should be defined in the municipalities, but we do not want to include this theme. I think there is an incoherent argument in this area. Perhaps the reality is that this is an expression of the fact that we, as political parties, believe that there are different things that it is important to ensure in this area. And I have to say that I think it is quite important that we take initiatives which mean that there are not many different employees walking in and out the door. And I actually think that we could do something here on the part of Christiansborg. Whether this is exactly the model proposed by the SF, I think we can discuss it. One could also define that it should be a theme that the municipalities described in their quality standards, without saying that it should be exactly this way. But, you know, the fact that you look up so loud and sacred that now you just want to protect local self-government, I think it's a little hollow when you don't, in other contexts at all, what to say, speak for it. So I would like to say to the SF: thank you for raising the debate. We are very interested in going ahead with it, also in the form of possible models other than those that lie here.

ParlaMint-DK_20170516233444	Thank you, sir. Rasmus Nordqvist. Then it is the rapporteur for the proposers, Mrs Kirsten Normann Andersen, SF.
ParlaMint-DK_20170516233451	Thank you. Thank you for a good and objective debate. We are talking a lot about dignified care for the elderly, and I think it is important to try to make some concrete suggestions about what that dignity could then be. I've been discussing a lot of outsourcing of care for the elderly with the Minister for the Elderly. I have also noted that there is some way to reaching agreement in this area. Yet it is actually the very same discussion that inspired this particular proposal. The Minister has repeatedly stressed that many citizens choose private companies because they can offer their citizens permanent helpers. I fully agree with the Minister that regular helpers are important for many older citizens. Indeed, it is so important that I think the Minister should consider how we can ensure that all citizens have permanent helpers, whether they choose a private company or whether they choose municipal home help. It almost goes without saying that it's just safer if it's your own home helper who's on duty. It can be extremely cross-border to rely on home help. In addition to helping the very personal things, it is not difficult to imagine that it can be unpleasant when others have to go into drawers and cupboards, perhaps without being able to join in. In time, however, most overcome the barriers as mutual trust is established between the helper and the citizen, but this naturally presupposes that it is about the same helpers who come to the home. It is quite true that the general reason for the elderly to choose a private company in the care of the elderly is firm helpers. Private companies are so aware of the perfectly reasonable desire that they often even advertise with that advantage. And precisely because it is so reasonable, it is also reasonable that we in the Folketing should discuss with each other why it is not right that every home care user could rightly expect to be satisfied because it is simply more worthy. As a number of rapporteurs have already mentioned, I also think that the municipalities would very much like to do so here. And when they have not long ago guaranteed the same rights for all citizens, there is probably also an explanation for this. Of course, there is a difference between how much continuity can be created for the individual citizen, depending on whether you get practical help every three weeks or you have 24-hour care. However, to the extent possible, I think the municipalities believe that the quality of home care can be improved in that particular area. It is therefore also paradoxical that a number of municipal politicians and leaders actually point to the law of free elections and forced competition as a factor in the continuity of sometimes giving way to rationality. Don't forget that more and more municipalities also point to budgets as a contributing factor. We measure so much else, so why not also see continuity as a quality parameter? For this is and remains a prerequisite for the success of the close health care system. Prevention in itself presupposes a thorough knowledge of the citizen concerned. It is therefore easier to see the changes if you know how the citizen normally feels. In the same way, however, security is also a very important prerequisite for how the citizen himself handles ills, prognostic sufferings, etc. Knowledge of the staff who may have to carry out more and more tasks in the home slowly, but surely, is important, and the situation is just easier to deal with if you are comfortable with the people who come to the home. If you are a little insecure about your own situation, it is safe and good that there will be some that you can turn things around with. It's just easier to work together on the citizen's own terms if you know each other well. It is simply easier to encourage a citizen if you know the background to the situation of the citizen. I would very much like to thank the rapporteurs for their positive attitude towards the aim of the proposal. I am pleased that we agree far along the way that continuity and quality often go hand in hand. For SF, it's not important how we achieve those goals. We are also open to other ways of achieving the same results. So I hope that we in the committee can try to continue working on finding good solutions to the fact that continuity is also a definition of quality in care for the elderly. Thank you very much for the debate and have a good sleep.
